# Reconstructing the engine — plan and progress

Start at [`../CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md) for the working practice. This file is
the roadmap and the running record of where the work has got to.

---

## 1. What "reconstruct" means here

Three levels, each a superset of the last. They are worth separating because
they need different amounts of work and only the first two are realistically
reachable by reading alone.

| level | definition | test |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Read** | every byte of shipped data is explained by a documented structure | a reader parses all of it with a self-checking invariant |
| **B. Explain** | every function that touches game state is named and its role stated | the call graph reads as a coherent design, no unexplained hubs |
| **C. Rebuild** | source that compiles and runs the game | it runs |

**Portability is a goal of level C, not an afterthought.** The replica is to
carry **no original assembly** — the binary and its listing are a *reference
and an oracle*, never a component. That single constraint decides several
things below: no static recompilation, no x87-shaped maths, and no reliance
on the 32-bit memory image the decompilation is written against.

**A is closed** (2026-08-28: every shipped file family has a verified parser
or an explicit "not established" note). **B is the working target** and is
well advanced across the game-logic subsystems. **C is now approachable, but
only through phase 6** — a headless simulator — not by writing engine code
from the decompilation directly: transcribing DirectX and C-runtime glue
teaches nothing about the game, and a compilable build that nothing can
test would drift from the original silently.

---

## 2. What the target actually is

2177 functions, 117566 lines. But not all of it is the game:

| group | funcs | lines | share | in scope? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| game / sys / file | 793 | 50228 | 42.7% | **yes — the core** |
| DirectX (d3d, ddraw, dsound, dinput) | 496 | 30182 | 25.7% | thin — identify, don't transcribe |
| win32 / OLE / MCI | 199 | 14995 | 12.8% | thin — mostly platform glue |
| script VM | 136 | 8465 | 7.2% | **yes — highest value per line** |
| o3de (3D engine) | 179 | 7498 | 6.4% | **yes** |
| C runtime (comlib) | 374 | 6198 | 5.3% | **no — compiler boilerplate, stub it** |

So the real objective is roughly **1100 functions / 66000 lines**, not 2177.
Recognising and excluding the runtime and the API wrappers is itself a task
(phase 0) because it makes every later percentage honest.

---

## 3. The method, and its limit

What has worked, every time: **the data is for finding, the code is for
confirming**, and a claim is only accepted when something can falsify it.
Concretely, for a data structure: re-implement the loader in Python, run it
against every shipped file, and require an invariant the data could fail —
lands exactly on the file size, pool consumed in order, every cross-reference
resolves, two independent chains agree.

**The limit:** that harness only exists where there is data to check against.
Roughly half the remaining code (input handling, window management, render
state) has no shipped artefact to verify a reading. There, the honest standard
drops from "verified" to "read and explained", and the `@status` model already
distinguishes those (`CLEAN`/`NAMED` vs `READ`). Do not let that distinction
blur to make progress look faster.

---

## 4. Phases

Ordered by (confidence achievable) × (value), not by size.

### Phase 0 — make the denominator honest — **done**

`tools/libcode.py` identifies the linked C/C++ runtime and writes
`readable/libcode.json`; `tools/status.py progress` now reports against game
code as well as the raw total.

**376 functions / 6216 lines from `0x004B3FB0`** are runtime, leaving **1801
functions / 111350 lines** of game code.

The boundary is *derived*, not chosen: sweeping the cut across the tail and
minimising inbound-call **density** lands on `0x004B3FB0`, where the block has
262 internal call edges and only **4** from outside. Corroborated
independently — that function is a `__thiscall` installing vtable pointers and
the next calls `operator delete`, i.e. the start of the C++ runtime.

Two things this cost, worth remembering:

* the obvious rule — "the contiguous tail the symbol pass could not name" —
  **overshoots badly**, reaching 0x004A4B30 and swallowing 59 functions that
  game code calls. Absence of a recovered name is weak evidence; the call graph
  is strong evidence.
* minimising the raw crossing *count* degenerates to a one-function block.
  Density is the right measure.

It is a list, not a `@status`, deliberately: it says "almost certainly not game
code", not "verified". The 4 residual crossings are recorded in the tool's
output and remain to be explained. The DirectX wrappers are **not** excluded —
they are thin but they are the game's own code.

### Phase 1 — the script VM *(136 functions, high value, best harness)*

**Done.** 127 of the 153 opcodes have a mnemonic; 114 of the 119 the world
scripts exercise are named — **99.97% of all executed instructions** — and the
five others are read with a recorded reason not to name (17 instructions in
all). The 26 opcodes with no mnemonic are the ones the shipped scripts never
execute; naming them is archaeology on dead code and can wait. This is
where understanding is cheapest per unit of code, because 5785 decoded scripts
are already available to test against.

* **Deliverable:** every exercised opcode named, with its operand domain
  confirmed; `docs/SCRIPT_VM.md` table completed.
* **Tests, all automatable:**
  1. operand length from the handler agrees with the corpus decode
     (`tools/vm_oplen.py` — already found 9 real errors);
  2. every operand is a valid index in the domain the handler logs — 100% for
     `VARIABLES`, `DIALOGS`, `OBJECTS`, `ADDRESSES`; note `ZONES.TAG` and
     `CAMERAS.TAG` are **incomplete** (ZONES covers 0..3531 with 573 gaps while
     operands reach 4551), so a sparse tag weakens this test rather than
     failing the opcode;
  3. the handler's callees — many are already-named engine functions;
  4. **the listing has to read as sensible game logic** — `tools/script_dump.py`
     renders any world script with every operand resolved through the `.TAG`
     tables. This is the check no invariant can make, and it earns its keep: its
     first run showed `OBJECTS[25493507]`, which is how the int16-field operand
     layout was found.
* **Then:** with the VM readable, the world scripts become a description of the
  game's logic, which feeds phase 3.

### Phase 2 — close out level A *(small)*

* ~~`IAM\OBJECT`~~ **solved** — the 1304-byte record fully decoded
  (FILE_FORMATS 5d): kind, flags, effect→actor-property mapping via
  `Object_ApplyEffect`, price, quantity, asset stem, name, description. Only
  `+2`=14 (one object) and the 15-vs-16 document split remain "not
  established".
* ~~`.CTL` flag bits~~ **read** — turn angles, stepping root-shifts, combat
  hit windows, an authoring move name, "no own clip" (only the last is
  code-confirmed); the `.3DM` `float[3]` **read but open** — the parser's
  integration is asm-confirmed, yet the corpus refutes delta semantics
  (near-constant in 57/60 files), so what neutralises the integral is the
  open question; node slots 0/1 narrowed to "unbound, not rotations".
* ~~the world chunks' unexplained bytes~~ — **accounted for** (2026-08-29,
  `tools/chunkmap.py`): header + eight tables + character strings + scripts
  claim everything in all 330 `AREA`/`SCENE` chunks bar **97 bytes** of
  unreferenced bytecode in three runs, with **0 table overlaps**. Two fields
  settled on the way (the object/prop `+8` is intra-record, not a table
  header; character `+0`/`+4` are the two strings). See
  [FILE_FORMATS](FILE_FORMATS.md), "Every byte of an AREA / SCENE chunk".
* **Test:** every remaining unexplained field either gets a structure or an
  explicit "not established" line in `docs/`.

### Phase 3 — the game core *(793 functions — the bulk)*

Take it subsystem by subsystem, and **prefer subsystems that own a data format**,
because those come with a test harness for free. Status (2026-08-28):

1. **actor / animation runtime** — **done.** The `.CTL` format is 100% read
   with every block's runtime consumer traced (transitions and the input
   bitfield with its real key bindings, combat, effects, special-move
   callbacks, turn/shift), the walker complete (gravity, collide-and-slide,
   ledges, conveyors, fall tiers), `Actors_TickAll` and the whole
   `ACTOR_STATE` map 0..17, dialogue mode, head look, morph gates.
2. **scene / area / trigger runtime** — **done.** The SCX object interpreter
   and camera editings, the zone records (quad + arc + save bit + camera),
   the event/message system, the zone-script lifecycle
   (enter/activate/leave), the script scheduler, area transitions.
3. **inventory and objects** — **done.** The `ObjectList_*` machinery, the
   prop-asset catalog, the interface text files, the save directory.
4. **combat and the sub-games** — **done.** Hit resolution, damage, throws,
   KO, the fight camera and HUD; the weapon-stats table; sliders and
   projectiles; "Sneak" turned out to be a UI, not a minigame. The **AI**
   closed 2026-08-30 ([`ASSETS.md`](ASSETS.md)): the fight AI is the `.CTL`'s
   `+76`/`+80` table — 156-byte profiles of button combos it injects into the
   player's own input queue — and the shoot AI is four hand-written callbacks
   picked by character type, with no data behind it at all.
* The phase-3 tail, as it stood — `Game_HandleEvent`'s inventory-screen
  cases, the I2D widget state machine, and the shoot/fight AI steps — is
  **done, all three, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md), [`ASSETS.md`](ASSETS.md)).

#### The UI — the "no harness" subsystem, and the next task

**This is the gate on a playable build**, and it is the one part of the game
where §3's limit really bites: there is no shipped file that says what a widget
does, and the golden traces cover the script layer, not the interface. So the
standard here is *read and explained*, and — per the porting refinement above —
this is the row that genuinely needs **CLEAN before porting**.

Measured 2026-08-30, by which functions touch the I2D / HUD primitives
(`I2D_SetState`, `I2D_SetFlag`, `I2D_SubmitQuad`, `I2D_ScaleX/Y`,
`Hud_ScaleX/Y`, `Text_DrawBlock`):

| | |
|---|---|
| functions touching them | **70** (5703 decompiler lines) |
| CLEAN | 2 |
| NAMED | 12 |
| **RAW — never read** | **56** |

What is **already read**, and is the frame to hang the rest on: `ui.open`
(opcode 70) opens one of 37 screens and **suspends the script at status 6**;
`Game_HandleEvent` case 5 writes the answer and resumes it; `UI_GridMenuInput`
+ `UI_SendAnswer` are the 7-slot menu widget in between (six in a 3-wide,
2-deep grid plus one apart — screen 29 `OMK START MENU` is that shape);
`UI_LoadScreen` fetches artwork as `I2d\bitmaps\%s` and its 11 names are
exactly the 11 files that ship; the interface **text** files are decoded
(FILE_FORMATS 5b4); the dialogue UI's phases 1–8 are read; and the HUD bars are
named.

**Start with the basics, not the AI.** In rough order: the I2D primitives
themselves, then the screen/widget state machine, then `Game_HandleEvent`'s
inventory cases. The largest unread bodies are `sub_4480D0` (464 lines),
`sub_492DA0` (331) and `sub_447000` (306). The shoot/fight AI is explicitly
**later** — it is the most data-poor part of an already data-poor subsystem.

**Done 2026-08-30 — see [`UI.md`](UI.md).** The I2D layer (display list,
seven primitives, the DirectDraw back end, the bitmap cache, the four flag
families), the screen system (the 92-byte table, the 3 slots, the
open/run/close state machine) and the whole options menu (74 items, 13 pages).
`tools/ui_tables.py`, `verify.py: ui tables`. Two corrections came out of it,
and both are worth carrying forward:

* **`I2D_SetState` was not the hub this plan took it for.** All 176 of its
  callers are page builders in one module, and what it does is bind a row of
  the options menu — it is now `Opt_BindRow`. The caller count was real; the
  inference from it was not.
* **Five functions in the options module are absent from `Runtime.exe.c`
  altogether**, the root page builder among them, and carry no `sub_` label in
  the listing either. Anything in this range that counts call sites in
  `readable/src` under-counts; `tools/asmfn.py <addr>` prints them.

**The widget drawing too, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md) §3b). `24_sys.c`'s
0x00475A50–0x0047ABA0 is the generic renderer for 34 of the 37 screens, and
reading it settles the widget tree — screen / panel / list / item, four levels
with four sets of flag words, which is why there are four flag-helper pairs.
The struct map is in `readable/types.h`; 32 more renames.

**And the text, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md) §5): `Text_LayOutBlock` and
`Text_DrawRun`, the 13-font table, the `.FNT` format (13/13) and the markup —
which corrected what `FILE_FORMATS` §5b4 had recorded about it.

**And the input path, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md) §3c) — which **corrected
this plan**: there are no per-screen input handlers. The definition table's
`+24` is `0x0042A0F0` for all 32 live screens, and the per-screen slots are
`+20` open and `+28` close.

**And the per-screen open/close callbacks, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md) §3d)
— which is where the UI subsystem's real obstacle turned out to be: **26 of
those 30 addresses have no function in `Runtime.exe.c` and no `proc` label in
the listing**, so `tools/asmfn.py` returns the wrong block for most of them and
they have to be disassembled from the image at their own address.

**And `Game_HandleEvent`'s inventory cases, 2026-08-30** ([`UI.md`](UI.md) §3e)
— 25..42, the data channel the interface asks an object list through, plus the
`GLOBAL +12` combination table.

What is left of the UI: the **panel and list bodies** each per-screen callback
installs — the same 13 functions still RAW in the renderer's range
(`sub_47A6D0` is `SAVE GAME`'s and reads as a worked example). With that, the
subsystem this plan called "the gate on a playable build" is read end to end
except those bodies.

The honest check to hold this work to, since the corpus cannot: every function
named must say *what evidence names it*, and anything that cannot be evidenced
keeps its address (CLAUDE.md §3). Where a screen's behaviour is visible in the
shipped data — the 37 screen ids, the 11 bitmaps, the text files, the 7-slot
layout — that is a real invariant and belongs in `verify.py`.

### Phase 4 — o3de *(179 functions — **the render path is done**)*

The 3D engine proper. So far: the load path (`Scene_Load3DO`,
`Read3DO_Init`, textures by extension flip), the scene graph ops
(link/unlink/free, traversals), the collision broad phase over the 184-byte
object records, and the renderer's topology — the acc3d/libdirect3d D3D
layer against the libpoly2d software rasterizers (identified from the
binary's own source paths, the rasterizers marked READ).

**The visible-set walk and the material/texture upload path are read
(2026-08-29)** — the two items this phase named as left, and they turned out
to be the same 14-bit number. `sub_48D3B0` culls a scene's flat node list by
a bounding sphere and four frustum planes, `Render_SubmitMesh` (0x004951C0)
bins each surviving face into one of **0x4000 buckets** keyed by
`meshState | material->textureSlot`, and `Render_FlushBuckets` (0x00460060)
walks the buckets in **ascending key order** into
`Raster_DrawTriangles(count, key)` — which binds
`g_D3DTextures[key & 0x3F]`. So the draw order *is* the key, and **the
texture is its low six bits**. The upload side is
`SetMaterialsMemory(58, 0)` → `Tex3DT_BindMaterials`: a global 58-slot pool
matched on the 19-char texture file name, stamped into material `+64` (which
ships as `-1`, proving it runtime), released by `Materials_ReleaseSlots`
under a scan of every resident scene. Full write-up in
[`docs/ASSETS.md`](ASSETS.md) §4b; it answers the Anekbah panel report
(CLAUDE.md §6) and corrects the alpha-blend reading.

**The lighting model is read too (2026-08-29)**, and the answer is that
there isn't one for sets: `sub_4947F0` copies the **BGRA colour dword** at
source vertex `+28` into the D3DTLVERTEX and modulates it by nothing, so
every set surface is shaded by an authored colour. The channel order is
settled by the software rasterizer's own grey fallback, which weights the
three bytes with the luma coefficients 299/587/114. The live pass exists
but is not for sets — `node+180` is set only by `LightObject`, called from
`Actor_LoadModel` and `Object_Load`, and its mesh-flag gate `0x8` is carried
by **0 of 15720** shipped meshes. Both viewers were rendering sets in
monochrome off the green byte and now carry the colour; see
[`ASSETS.md`](ASSETS.md) §4c.

**The sprite path closed it (2026-08-29).** `Render_Frame` (0x00441030) is
the per-frame entry — clear the buckets, walk the meshes, then
`Render_SubmitSprites` (0x004969C0) and two more passes, then flush — and the
sprite pass puts camera-facing billboards into the **same bucket array under
the same key**, with the blend mode from the instance's `+20`. So the whole
route from a `.3DO` face or an effect sprite to a pixel is read, and the
effects chain built on it runs in `/cutscene` (ASSETS §3b).

Left in phase 4: the **304-byte light records** at `scene[8]` (169 in
ANEKBAH) — only the four fields `sub_493E40` reads are known — the
28-byte records at `scene[10]` that bind node sets together (53 in ANEKBAH,
what `mesh3do` calls "doors"), and the two unread render passes `sub_496FC0`
and `sub_48D7F0`. Nothing currently drawn needs any of them. The libpoly2d rasterizers stay READ, and so
does the whole second back end: nothing shipped selects it
(`off_4C4918[1]` and its four siblings are never installed; see the READ
banner on `sub_42FF80`), and neither is the environment-map pass —
`dword_4C9520`, the only texture stage 1 is ever given, is set to `-1` at
init and never assigned.

### Phase 6 — the headless simulator *(the bridge from B to C)*

**All five stages done, plus a sixth added later — the area load.** This is
what turns level B into level C, and it is also the only honest test of level
B: a name is an opinion until something runs on it. Stage 6 is the case in
point: it was not missing from the plan because it was hard, but because
nobody had noticed the engine runs a script the corpus walk never listed. Stage 2 was taken first because it was the stated
blocking deliverable; stage 1 followed, and with it **the behavioural harness
this project had never had** — `verify.py: sim: VM executes`.

The target is a **Python simulator that reuses `tools/` wholesale** — no
rendering, no audio, no input, no window. It boots `START` and
`aventure.scx`, advances a fixed 30 fps clock, and runs the script
scheduler, the VM and the SCX object interpreter against a real game-state
model. Everything that would draw or make noise is a **stub that records the
call**, because the trace is the product: what the game *decides* is what we
are trying to reproduce, not what it displays.

Why Python and not the engine directly: every parser already exists here and
is corpus-checked, an unimplemented opcode fails loudly instead of hiding,
and the result is an executable specification a compilable engine can be
ported *from* rather than guessed at. Writing C++ first would mean debugging
two unknowns at once.

#### What the numbers say about the size of it

The world scripts are **5785 slots, 58644 instructions (58428 since 2026-09-02, when op 62's operand length went from the table's 4 to the handler's 6 and its 216 phantom `dbg`/`nop` instructions vanished), 119 distinct
opcodes**, and they all decode today. (Scoped as 5775 / 57185 / 118 — that
count predated the `GLOBAL` slots being folded into the same scan, and the
figures here are re-measured.) The opcodes split sharply by what they
demand of a simulator:

* about **fifteen** carry the control flow and need real semantics —
  `end`, `push.i8/i16/var`, `set.var/var2`, `jmp`, `jmp_if_false`, `case`,
  `cmp.*`, `and/or`. Ten of them alone are 52% of all executed instructions;
* a second small group needs only **bit arrays** — `zone.enable/disable`,
  `character.show/hide`, the ADDRESSES bitmap;
* everything else — `camera.set` (3659 uses), `media.play`, `scx.play*`,
  `dialog.start`, `actor.goto_address`, `area.goto`, `player.move` — is a
  **subsystem call that a headless run can stub and log**. Control flow stays
  correct without any of them implemented.

So the first milestone is small, and the expensive parts are the two named
below, not the opcode count.

#### The stages, each with a test the run can fail

1. **VM core + variables + flag arrays — done.** `tools/sim/vm.py`: the ~20
   flow opcodes for real against the stage-2 `GameState`, everything else
   stubbed and traced. The semantics come from the handlers, not the corpus —
   one shared 16-bit operand fetch with the `0x4000` indirect bit through the
   params table at `+36`; branch targets relative to the pc *after* the
   operand; `jmp_if_false` pops, `case` **peeks** and jumps when the label
   does *not* match. Two corrections fell out: **op 18 is `set.var.pop`**, not
   the `set.var.bit` the table said (it pops and calls `Var_Set`), and
   `case`'s target is measured **before** the label byte.
   *Test:* all **5785** slots execute — 5645 to `end`, 140 stopping at
   `dialog.start` where `Script_Execute` returns outright — with 0 failures
   and **0 pcs outside their own slot's instruction stream**. That last
   invariant is the one with teeth: "inside the buffer" passes trivially,
   because a chunk holds its scripts back to back and a target one byte out
   lands in a real instruction of the neighbour. It is what caught `case`
   (4 unknown-opcode slots, 41 `drop`s onto an empty stack). 118 distinct
   opcodes executed, 33918 instructions — fewer than the 58644 the
   disassembler walks, because execution skips the untaken arms.
2. **The game-state schema — done.** [`GAME_STATE.md`](GAME_STATE.md) and
   `tools/gamestate.py`. It is one 8192-byte `Mem_Calloc`, and `IAM\START`
   is that block as shipped — the new-game save, not a container. The layout
   is `State_Apply`'s own arithmetic; the six arrays it points at (694
   variables, 259 areas, 670 prop states, 1032 object bits, 791 address bits,
   4558 zone bits) **tile the file to the byte**, each count agreeing with an
   independently established number, and no bit is set past one. The clock is
   the other half: two globals, saved beside the block, on a 41-day /
   13-month / year-7216 calendar the shipped newspapers date themselves in.
   `GameDB` is written into `readable/types.h`, which is what a port needs.
   *Test:* `verify.py: game state` — the walk lands on 5686 = the file size,
   the six counts agree with six other sources, 0 of 3 spare tail bits are
   set, and `State_Apply` → `State_Save` round-trips byte-for-byte except the
   8 bytes the loader is known to clobber. Plus `verify.py: game clock`.
3. **Scheduler, zones and events — done.** `tools/sim/world.py`: the zone
   records read as the runtime sees them (quad, facing arc, save bit), the
   registration filter, the prompt slots, the per-context action FIFO and the
   pump that drains it — `Zones_RegisterAll`, `Script_Pump` and
   `Script_ProcessActions` transcribed.
   *Test:* placing the player inside zone **3732** fires that zone's activate
   script, and the trace is the documented launch by name — `zone.disable
   3732`, `player.anim.hold`, `scx.play.player`, `character.look_at_player`,
   `dialog.start 387`. The **lifecycle closes**: the zone's save bit goes
   1 → 0 and `Zones_RegisterAll` no longer registers it, which is what "a
   spent trigger stays retired" means. That last part needed the bit-array
   opcodes for real, and `zone.disable` only works because it is implemented
   from the **assembly** — `Zone_SetStateBit` decompiles as a bare OR that
   could never clear a bit.
4. **SCX object interpreter and the dialogue runtime — done.**
   `tools/sim/scene.py` ticks `Script_PlayScript`: the pc, the sync chain, the
   per-function repeat limits, the loop count, the shared clock and the
   linked-pair hand-over. `tools/sim/run.py` wires `scx.play*` and
   `dialog.start` to the real scene, so a launch stops being a trace and
   becomes an execution.
   *Test, both halves of the plan's own:* `Telis_eat` alternates
   `TELRES05` / `TELRES02` for ever and never stops; and dialog **387**'s
   launch runs end to end — `player.anim.hold` → `scx.play.player.wait` →
   `scx.play.player` → `character.look_at_player` → `dialog.start 387` — with
   SCENE 53 resolving to `Re14.SCX` through AREA 217's `+97`, objects 22 and
   32 resolving to `Uzal---->assis` and `Uzal_Stand`, 387 loading as
   `Telis/Déjeûner` with 13 nodes, and **one of the two programs still
   running** after 120 frames: the entrance must finish, the loop need not.
5. **Actor and walker — done, with one part explicitly not done.**
   `tools/sim/actor.py`: gravity with the 20 m/s clamp, the ground probe, the
   30 cm step limit up and down, the ledge refusal, the 3 m / 5 m fall tiers,
   and a `.CTL` channel running `Cef_FindTransition`'s search.
   *Test:* walking a spiral across `ARESTO14` from an authored `ADDRESSES`
   position, the actor **moves where there is floor and reverts where there
   is not** (287 / 113 over 400 steps), his height never changes and he never
   falls; and pressing `0x04` walks `H_STAND → H_SD-WK → H_WALK`, every
   consecutive pair an edge the file carries.
   ~~**Not modelled:** the narrow phase.~~ **Done 2026-08-29** — the debt this
   stage shipped with ("the actor keeps to the floor but walks through walls").
   The chain is `Sweep_ActorMove` (0x004AD360) → `Sweep_MeshTest` (0x004AD460)
   → `Sweep_MeshFaces` (0x004A9AB0) → `Sweep_PolygonKernel` (0x004A9D30), then
   `Walk_ClampNormal` (0x0046A020), three passes. Two things the reading
   corrected: `Sweep_MeshTest` filters by **mesh flag** (0x20000000, 0x41), not
   by slope, so the sweep's face set is *every* collision face rather than the
   walkable soup the ground probe uses; and **`Walk_ClampNormal` is not a plane
   slide** — a 6-bit mask says which axis directions are already blocked, those
   components of the normal are zeroed and the rest renormalised, which is what
   lets three passes take an inside corner.

   **Where the line is drawn, and it is drawn rather than blurred.**
   `clamp_normal` is a transcription. The sweep is not: `Sweep_PolygonKernel`
   is **930 lines** of x87 geometry and this tree holds no shipped fact a
   transcription could be proved against — the bar every other kernel here was
   held to (ADPCM, the quaternion maths, `Path_Sample`). So
   `tools/sim/actor.py` implements the same algorithm *shape* — earliest hit of
   a swept sphere, clamp, slide, three passes — over the same faces, with the
   face case continuous and edges/vertices left to a static test, and the
   radius a stand-in because the engine derives it per model from the collision
   spheres at model +260 times a runtime tunable. Both functions are read and
   named; the kernel's banner records why it was left as generated.

   *Test:* `find_partition` picks the biggest vertical face with **floor on
   both sides** whose y range contains the sphere centre — most walls have no
   floor behind them, so the ground probe already refuses them and a test there
   would pass with no narrow phase at all. Walking straight at that partition:
   **sweep off ends 140 units behind the wall** (verdict `moved`), **sweep on
   ends 28 in front** (verdict `blocked`). `verify.py: sim: narrow phase`. The
   floor test keeps its own run at `radius=0`, since turning the sweep on
   changes the path and would otherwise answer a different question with the
   same numbers.

6. **The area load — added 2026-08-29, after stages 1-5.** Not in the original
   stage list, and its absence was invisible until `AREA +4` / `SCENE +4` were
   found: the simulator had **one** entry point, `enter_zone`, so it could
   stand the player in a trigger but had no notion of an area *arriving*. It
   therefore never ran the startup scripts — 1968 instructions of
   initialisation the engine runs before any trigger exists, and what every
   session of play begins with. `Session.load_area()` is `Area_TickLoad`
   (0x0040C7E0) case 9 transcribed: the AREA block's `+4` into
   `Script_NewContext` and `Script_QueueAction(ctx,1)`, then the SCENE's if the
   game DB says one is over it, then `Zones_RegisterAll`.

   Two things it forced, both real rather than scaffolding: VM opcode 71
   `scene.load` now **writes** `gameDB+12` (`Area_SetLoadedScene`) instead of
   being stubbed, because `Area_TickLoad` reads it straight back to decide
   whose startup script to run — so which scene is over an area is save state;
   and a context's script offsets are relative to its own chunk, so the pump
   carries a **buffer per resident chunk** (`World.buffers` / `code_for`).

   *Test — the engine, not an invariant.* AREA 118 is "Introduction Kay'l",
   what a new game enters, and `traces/intro.log` is the original running it.
   All **7** decisions the sim announces are the capture's **first 7,
   contiguously and in order**: variables 175 and 170, `media.play` 997, the
   `Interface` question, cameras 2172 and 2148, conversation 272.
   `verify.py: sim: area load`.

   **The conversation returns control, and the area transition is chained**
   (both added the same day), so the run reaches **trace event 42 of 58** and
   reproduces every one of the capture's first 42 **in order, with no gaps at
   all** — 45 announcements, 42 confirmed, nothing skipped. The last hole,
   event 20, closed when `inventory.add`'s announced field was read from its
   handler (below). That is the game's whole opening: the intro,
   conversation 272, `area.goto 222`, `scene.load(222, 55)`, and SCENE 55's
   startup script firing the **sixteen Impasse beats** before handing off to
   Kay'l's apartment.

   **The transition, as the engine does it.** `area.goto` is `Area_Transition`
   case 0 — it stages a load into the other slot and parks its caller at
   **status 10**, which `Script_ProcessActions` refuses to touch;
   `Script_Pump`'s tail finishes it once `Area_TickLoad` reports done, and only
   then does the caller resume after its `area.goto`. Getting that wrong shows
   up at once: without the park the intro runs straight past, `scene.load`
   fires before the area exists, and SCENE 55's startup script starts **twice**
   — once from `scene.load` and once from `Area_TickLoad` case 9 reading the
   state the first had just written. `scene.load` is itself a launcher (op 71's
   handler ends `Scene_Block` → `[ebx+4]` → `Script_NewContext` →
   `Script_QueueAction`), which is why the beats need no transition of their
   own.

   **Pacing** (added the same day) makes the run take **2220 frames** to reach
   event 42 where it used to take a handful — and it is the game's own
   mechanism, not a delay. Seven of SCENE 55's sixteen beats use a *waiting*
   variant (`scx.play.player.wait` 46, `scx.play.wait` 58,
   `scx.play.actor.wait` 60), and those pass the caller's **slot** to
   `ScriptObject_Start` instead of the `-1` op 57 passes, register the object
   in the wait slots at `dword_910500/4/8`, and end `mov [esi+16h], 4` —
   parking the caller at status **4**. `Game_Tick` then runs, every frame:

   ```c
   if (*v9 && !ScriptObject_IsBusy(*v9)) { *v9 = 0; Game_RaiseEvent(3, v8); }
   ```

   and `Game_HandleEvent` case 3 puts a status-4 context back to 1. **A beat
   finishing is what advances the cutscene**, and the SCX interpreter's own
   busy spans (clip frames, `Script_Wait`) supply the durations. `Session.tick`
   and `Session.frame` are now one path, in `Game_Tick`'s order: contexts,
   then programs, then the wait scan.

   It also reordered the run in a way a fixed driver could not have predicted —
   the intro parks on a `scx.play.actor.wait` **before** it ever reaches
   `dialog.start` — so the harness now closes a conversation when the world is
   actually blocked on one rather than at a scripted point.

   **Two limits, stated rather than tuned.** The *absolute* timings are not the
   engine's: the capture spends 35 s in the start menu and 110 s in
   conversation 272, neither modelled because a person is doing both, and the
   Impasse itself runs 43 s here against the engine's 69 s, because
   `_busy_span` returns 0 for paths and sync cues. And the ceiling is still
   event 42 — the capture's 43–58 are the tutorial, which needs the **player to
   walk** into zones 3795/3796 and area 142. That is movement, not pacing, and
   it is where the narrow phase (stage 5's stated debt) comes back.

   `dialog.start` is **not a suspend**, and that had to be read rather than
   guessed. Its handler writes no status and the interpreter simply does
   `if (v4 == 61) return;`, so the context is left *running* with its pc past
   the operands. What blocks is the world: `Dialog_Load` writes
   `g_DialogState = 3` and `Script_Pump` case 1 opens `if (g_DialogState != 1)
   return 1;` — the whole per-frame step, prompt slots and `Script_Execute`
   included, stops until `Game_HandleEvent` case 63 unloads the conversation
   and writes 1 back.

   **The capture is what settled it.** A resume-next-frame model is
   indistinguishable from the real one in event *order*; only the timestamps
   separate them — conversation 272 at t=41.4 s, the next camera at
   t=151.5 s, 110 seconds later. So the sim splits `Script_ProcessActions`
   from `Script_Execute`, keeps each context's own VM (its stack has to
   survive the yield), separates the context table from the prompt slots, and
   gates the pump.

   **What is still supplied rather than derived** is a player's answer — but
   the mechanism around it is now the engine's. `ui.open` (SCRIPT_VM 70)
   stores the result variable's index in `dword_4E6B28` and parks its caller
   at status **6**, and `Game_HandleEvent` **case 5** writes the answer into
   that variable and returns the context to 1, its argument block carrying the
   context index at `+4` and the chosen value at `+8`. The simulator does
   exactly that: the intro suspends on screen 29 `OMK START MENU` asking for
   variable 19, and the harness answers **1** — the value `verify.py: save
   file` reads out of a real save. Replacing the old pre-seed with the real
   suspend/answer **changed no number**, which is the point: the same run, with
   the supplied input localised to the one call where the engine asks for it.
   How long a conversation lasts is likewise not modelled; `run_dialog()` is
   the caller saying it ended.

   **Two corrections it forced, both worth more than the feature.** `loggable`
   named field 0 as the announced operand where a handler logs a later one
   (op 71 pushes field 1, op 52 its second operand — read from the handlers);
   and the attribution corpus was the 5785 slots, which omit the `+4` startup
   scripts. With both fixed the corpus is **5958** — all executing, 0 failures,
   0 strayed pcs — and the capture's `SCENES/55`, `ADDRESSES/654` and
   `AREAS/118` become uniquely owned by AREA 118's startup script, with
   `SCENE 55 rec -1 +4`, the Impasse driver, attributed and agreeing.

   **And it left one residue, since CLOSED.** `trace agreement`'s single
   disagreement was `OBJECTS/314` ("Memo 001 Intro"), owned in the corpus only
   by `AREA 237 rec 24 +0` — five instructions with **no branches**, so had it
   run all three of its events would be in the capture; only one was. Two
   wrong turns on the way, both worth keeping: a "second site" in `SCENE 63` at
   offset 1553 was a byte pattern inside that chunk's camera table (the
   byte-accounting pass caught it), and the conclusion that the emitter must be
   *outside* the corpus was itself wrong. It was a **fourth** field-map bug:
   `inventory.add` (op 50) announces its SECOND operand, and the emitter is
   `inventory.add(2, 314)` at pc 1215 of **SCENE 55's startup script** — a
   script the corpus had held all along. Both captures now replay with **zero
   disagreements**.

7. **The player as an actor — added 2026-08-29, and the furthest the run
   goes.** Stage 6 reached trace event 42 with the player as a *teleport*:
   `enter_zone` dropped him inside a quad. The capture's 43–58 are the
   tutorial, and they fire because a person **walks** into a trigger, so
   `Session.spawn_player` now puts a real `Walker` on the area's set (`AREA
   +88`) at an authored `ADDRESSES` position, carrying the narrow phase from
   stage 5, and `Session.frame` feeds his position and facing to
   `Script_Pump`'s zone scan instead of pumping blind. Walking him into zone
   3795 fires its enter script — the tutorial — and the run reaches **trace
   event 55 of 58**, 49 confirmed in order.

   **The path is supplied, and that is the honest half.** Where the player
   walked is player input; it is not in the data and nothing here recovers it.
   What is tested is the other half — that walking there produces the engine's
   decisions in the engine's order, through the real zone lifecycle.

   **It found a unit bug the moment an actor and a zone shared a frame.** The
   zone quad is stored in *authoring* units and the loaders convert it:
   `(100 * v) * 0.00390625 * 0.3937… − 1` over the four corners' x and z, and
   the same with a further **−9** over their y. `tools/sim` read them raw,
   which is self-consistent while nothing else is in the frame — the zone tests
   place the player at the zone's own centre — so it survived until an actor at
   an authored address came out **43000 units** from the zone he was standing
   in. Converted, **all 12** of AREA 222's zone records land inside AIMPASSE's
   own bounding box and the player spawns between two of them; that is the
   invariant the raw reading fails.

   **The crossing into area 142 now happens too**, the way the game does it:
   zone **3801**'s enter script is an `area.goto 142`, so walking into that
   quad runs `Area_Transition` case 0, parks the context at status 10, and
   `Area_TickLoad` case 9 brings the area up and re-registers. Area 142's zones
   (2319, 2320, 2329) become resident and so does **3796** — whose save bit is
   0 at new game, is set by zone **3791**'s script on the way past, and which
   only goes live once `Zones_RegisterAll` rebuilds. That rebuild has three
   call sites, all area load or transition, none per-frame, so nothing but the
   crossing could have made it live. Five zones fire; the run reaches **trace
   event 56 of 58**.

   **The route is derived, not hand-written.** `nav_route` breadth-first
   searches a 16-unit grid whose edges are legal only when `Walker.step`
   returns "moved" *and lands in the cell it aimed at* — the shortest route the
   walker itself can take over the collision geometry. The arrival test is not
   a detail: without it a step that slides along a wall counts as reaching the
   neighbour, and the search returns routes straight through buildings, which
   is what it did first.

   Five events stay unmatched for an **ordering** reason rather than a missing
   mechanism: the derived route to zone 3803 passes through 3795 first, so the
   tutorial fires before 3803's `media.play`, and an in-order match cannot then
   take 43/44/45/46 or the second 3796 at 51. Which way the capture's player
   actually walked is player input and is not in the data. **Nothing in the
   capture is unexplained any more.** `verify.py: sim: tutorial walk`.

8. **Conversations execute — added 2026-08-29, the last stub in the decision
   path.** `dialog.start` loaded a conversation and the harness then declared
   it over; `tools/sim/dialogue.py` runs it. Node by node: evaluate each
   branch's condition, take one, execute that branch's action against the same
   `GameState` everything else reads, follow `param[k]` to the next node.
   `Session.run_dialog` wires it in, so the opening now *has* the Kay'l/Intro
   conversation rather than skipping it.

   The two halves of a node's nine pointers were **proven by tracing** rather
   than guessed: event 55, fired while `Dialog_TickUI` builds the reply menu,
   evaluates `ptr[0..3]` through `Dialog_EvalBranchCondition` and takes the
   value; event 59, fired when a reply is chosen, executes `ptr[4..7]` through
   `Dialog_GetBranchAction`. Conditions gate, actions run.

   *Tests, three of them, each one the run can fail:*
   * **all 321 conversations reach a leaf** — 0 cycles, 0 failures, 0 hitting
     the step limit. Nothing in the format forbids a cycle in the node graph,
     so termination is observed with a visited guard rather than assumed;
   * **all 612 dialogue scripts execute to `end`** — 247 conditions and 365
     actions, the dialogue's own corpus on the same VM the 5958 world scripts
     run on;
   * **all 1452 branch targets are valid node indices** — independently the
     number [FILE_FORMATS](FILE_FORMATS.md) quotes from the format work,
     arrived at here by executing rather than by parsing.

   Which reply a person picks is player input and is not in the data; the
   default policy takes the first available branch, which is the least
   interesting one that still exercises every condition. How long a
   conversation lasts is still not modelled. `verify.py: sim: dialogue`.

#### How much of the original code can be reused

Measured, because the answer is not uniform. The decompilation as a whole is
**not compilable**: 306 `__asm` blocks, 324 pseudo-register references, 36
`__usercall` prototypes, 1299 partial-register macros — and, more seriously,
**84 sites where the decompiler says a conditional was "optimized away"** and
204 "possibly undefined" variables. Those last two are places where the C is
known to be *semantically incomplete*, so it cannot be trusted even where it
compiles. Per function, though, most of that is absent, which makes reuse a
per-function decision rather than an all-or-nothing one.

Four tiers, in descending order of value:

1. **Tables and constants — reuse freely, as data.** The camera presets, the
   66-row `tab_special_move`, the weapon stats, the key bindings, the `.CTL`
   role codes, the opcode operand lengths, the ADPCM tables. These are facts
   about the game rather than code, they are already extracted into `docs/`
   with checks behind them, and in a replica they are simply data files.
   **Refined 2026-08-30, and it moves the estimate a long way.** "CLEAN before
   porting" is a rule about *risk*, not a rule about provenance: it exists so
   that complex, specific behaviour is not transcribed before it is understood,
   because a dropped conditional there is silent. It does **not** apply where
   the port can be *proved* independently of the decompiled body. For a file
   parser the proof is the corpus, not the C: level A is closed, every shipped
   format has a reader with an invariant the data could fail, and a fresh C
   implementation is validated the same way — byte-identical output against the
   Python over every shipped file. The same is true of tier 3's kernels, and,
   since phase 6, of a surprising amount of *behaviour*: the VM has 5785 slots
   that execute, and the golden traces replay 43 scripts with 0 disagreements.

   So the porting surface is not "the 64 CLEAN functions". It is:

   | | port how | proof |
   |---|---|---|
   | data tables | lift as data | already checked in `docs/` |
   | pure kernels | write fresh | differential, against the shipped corpus |
   | **every file format** | **write fresh** | **the corpus, exactly as the Python readers are** |
   | VM + scene/actor runtime | write fresh, carefully | `tools/sim` and the golden traces |
   | no harness (input, render state, fight AI, the 84 dropped conditionals) | **CLEAN first** | reading only — the rule's real domain |
   | DirectX / win32 / runtime / rasterizers | do not port | n/a |

   Only the fifth row needs a CLEAN body before it can be written, and that is
   also the row where §3's limit already says the standard drops to "read and
   explained". CLEAN stays the bar for *that* code and for anything a harness
   cannot reach.

2. **Port from the CLEAN bodies — the intended path** *for behaviour without a
   harness; see the refinement above.* The 63 hand-rewritten
   functions (5479 decompiler-lines) are the closest thing to a specification
   of the engine's behaviour, produced to this project's standard with a
   banner recording the evidence: the script interpreter, `GoToMove`, the hit
   resolver, the actor tick, the scheduler, the loaders. **Every function that
   matters should become CLEAN before it is ported, not after.**

   They are a *specification*, though, not portable code. They still address
   the original 32-bit memory image through offset accessors — about **15100**
   `u32(p, 12)`-style reads across the tree — so a port replaces those with
   real fields. That is what `readable/types.h` is for: **17 structures** are
   already named field by field with their corroboration, and CLEAN body plus
   struct together is the artefact to port. Where a structure is still only
   offsets, writing it into `types.h` is the first step of porting it.
3. **Transcribe pure kernels one at a time, with a differential test.** The
   maths and codecs — ADPCM, `Matrix3x3_FromQuaternion` /
   `FromEulerAngles` / `RotateVector`, `Path_Sample`'s interpolation, the
   quaternion composition. They are pure input→output, so a port can be
   *proved* against the shipped corpus exactly the way the parsers are
   (296177 quaternions, 2534/2534 textures). This is the ground rule of §3
   applied to code instead of formats.
4. **Do not reuse at all:** the six x87 rasterizers (already READ with a
   reason, and a replica may render however it likes), the DirectX / win32 /
   OLE glue, the linked C runtime — and *anywhere the decompiler flags
   dropped semantics*, where `Runtime.exe.asm` is the only honest source.

#### Where the code lives — three trees, and when the third starts

Decided 2026-08-28, because the boundary is easier to keep than to restore.

| tree | what it is | compiled |
|---|---|---|
| `readable/` | the decompilation, hand-cleaned, `@status`-tracked | **never** |
| `tools/sim/` | the Python executable specification — this phase | no; it runs |
| `engine/` | the portable replica | yes, **when phase 6 can test it** |

`readable/` is a *specification*, not source: ~15100 offset accessors against
the original 32-bit memory image, 306 `__asm` blocks, and 84 sites the
decompiler admits it dropped a conditional from. It is never built.

**`engine/` STARTED 2026-08-30.** Its first slice is the `.3DT` textures:
a C++20 reader written fresh from the format, building clean under
`-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic`, and **2534 of 2534 textures byte-identical to
`tools/tex3dt.py`** (`verify.py: engine 3DT`, --slow). That is the tier-1
pattern working as designed - the proof is the corpus, so the port needed no
CLEAN body and touched no original assembly.

Two things the slice established beyond the textures. **The original is not
C++**: of 432 `__thiscall` functions, 422 are the linked runtime and 9 are
game code, with no vtables, no exceptions and effectively no `std::` - it is
C built with a C++ toolchain, so the replica's language and paradigm are a
free choice rather than a departure. And a **stale claim was corrected on the
way**: `tex3dt.py`'s docstring still described the codec as a best fit
landing exactly on about a third of textures, which had not been true for
some time - `verify.py: textures` had been asserting 2534/2534 all along.
Porting from a description is a good way to find out the description is old.

The note below is kept because its reasoning still governs everything after
the file readers.

**`engine/` was deliberately not created before this.** A compilable tree with no
harness drifts from the original silently — the reason this phase exists at all
— and an empty one sitting beside `readable/` is an invitation to paste from
it, which is exactly what the no-original-assembly constraint forbids. It
starts when the simulator can diff against it, and its rule is: **written from
a CLEAN body plus its `readable/types.h` struct, never copied from
`readable/`.** Turning the offsets into fields *is* the port. Tier 3's pure
kernels are the exception and can go early, since they prove against the
shipped corpus on their own.

One thing `engine/` will need that nothing had extracted: the tables compiled
into `Runtime.exe`. A replica requiring the user's own game data will not find
those in `gamedata/`. **Done 2026-08-30** — `tools/exetables.py` writes six of them
to `tables/*.json` (the VM dispatch table, `tab_special_move`, the camera
presets, the ADPCM step/index tables, the four default control schemes, the
three UI tables), each with a check its own data could fail, and
`verify.py: exe tables` re-derives and diffs so a stale file cannot pass. The
"second copy to drift" objection is what that check answers.

Two corrections it produced. **The weapon stats are not a compiled table** —
`Weapon_SlotForObject` indexes `IAM\GLOBAL +32`, which ships, as do the
fight-AI profiles (`.CTL` +76/+80) and the combination table (`GLOBAL +12`); a
replica reads those from the user's data and lifting them *would* have been the
second copy. And the VM lift **fills a hole rather than duplicating one**: op
2's handler is 0 in `clean/_vmsummary.json`, because the extraction pass could
not bound its block, where the image gives `0x00401B80`. 152 of the other 153
agree exactly.

#### The assembly's best use is as the oracle, not as the engine

Static recompilation — assembling `Runtime.exe.asm` and shimming its imports —
would reproduce behaviour exactly, but it teaches nothing, it is x86-32
forever, and it violates the portability goal outright. The far better use of
the same effort: **run the original to generate golden traces**, and diff the
simulator against them. That is precisely the missing behavioural harness
named below, and it costs a logging shim rather than a port.

**Diff decisions, not numbers.** The original computes on the x87 stack with
80-bit intermediates; any portable replica will use doubles or SSE and drift
in the low bits immediately. A trace comparison must therefore be at the
level of *what the game decided* — which script ran, which opcode with which
operand, which state bit changed, which dialog started, which state a channel
entered — with floats compared under a tolerance and never for equality. The
fixed 30 fps step (`Game_Frame`'s dt in 30 fps units) is what makes even that
much reproducible.

**Two more consequences of "no original assembly".** File "pointers" are not
pointers: the `.CTL` and `.SCX` images are saved 32-bit memory dumps whose
pointer fields the loaders overwrite, so a portable reader must treat them as
offsets and never as native addresses — a 64-bit build that assumes otherwise
breaks silently. And every format is little-endian by construction, so
readers must decode explicitly rather than punning structs over the buffer.

One practical note on what gets *distributed* rather than run locally: the
safest and most common pattern in this field is code that carries no original
material and requires the user's own game data. Tier 2's hand-written bodies
sit at that end; verbatim assembly sits at the other.

#### The risk that is not in the stage list — now half answered

**There was no behavioural harness, and formats had one from day one.**
`verify.py` could not tell whether a simulated conversation went the way the
game would. Two candidates were named, in order of value: golden traces from
the original, or invariant runs over the whole corpus in the spirit of
`verify.py`. **Stage 1's test is deliberately the second kind**, and it now
exists — `sim: VM executes` and its three siblings — so the harness was in
place before any engine code.

The first kind is still worth having, and the practical constraint is now
known rather than assumed.

##### Running the original: what it takes on an arm64 Mac

The parenthetical that used to sit here — "it is a 1999 Win32 binary and it
runs" — is **false on Apple Silicon**. `Runtime.exe` is 32-bit x86, and
**Rosetta 2 translates x86-64 only**; macOS dropped i386 in Catalina. No build
of vanilla Wine fixes that, because the missing piece is below Wine.

Two routes, and neither is a blocker for the reason the plan already gives:

* **CrossOver**, which carries a 32-on-64 path for exactly this case;
* **FEX-Emu + Wine inside an arm64 Linux VM**, which emulates i386 natively
  and, being an emulator rather than a compatibility fork, is easier to reason
  about if fidelity is ever in doubt.

**Does a translation layer spoil the trace? Mostly no, and the design already
says why.** "Diff decisions, not numbers" was adopted because *our own*
replica drifts from x87 in the low bits; a translator introduces the same
class of discrepancy, not a new one. Tier the trace and the exposure is
visible:

| trace | made of | at risk |
|---|---|---|
| opcode, operand, script slot, variable writes | **integers end to end** | no |
| zone lifecycle, state bits, dialog ids, `.CTL` state ids | integers | no |
| walker positions, animation blending, timing | x87 floats | low bits |

The first two tiers — most of what a golden trace is for, and where the
simulator is strongest — cannot be affected at all. The real exposure is a
**decision that hinges on a float comparison near a boundary**: a cancel
window (`frame < cancelStart`), the 30° slope test, the 30 cm step limit. A
mismatch there is ambiguous between our bug and the translator's rounding.

**Bound that before building anything:** run one save with one input twice
under the chosen layer and diff the two captures against each other. If the
original is not self-consistent there, that is an hour spent instead of a
pipeline wasted.

##### The rig, as built — `tools/goldentrace.py` — **working**

    python3 tools/goldentrace.py setup --create     # bottle + shim, once
    python3 tools/goldentrace.py run --seconds 90   # play; it records
    python3 tools/goldentrace.py parse traces/<log> # -> the decision stream
    python3 tools/goldentrace.py diff  traces/<log> # against tools/sim
    python3 tools/goldentrace.py run --keys return,down,return   # scripted

**Scripted input, and two macOS permissions that fail silently.** `--keys`
plays a sequence into the running game through System Events, addressed to the
Wine process by its own name — the game appears as an ordinary foreground
process called `Runtime 2.exe`. It is what a **menu** capture needs, since a
menu is only visible as a suspension and a branch (`docs/UI.md` §3f). Two
permissions gate it and both look like the game ignoring input:

* **Accessibility** — without it `osascript` returns error **1002**, "not
  allowed to send keystrokes". It is granted to the *terminal*, not to this
  script, and `send_keys` prints the refusal rather than swallowing it.
* **Screen Recording** — unrelated to keys, but without it `screencapture`
  answers "could not create image from display", so you cannot look at the
  menu to see what happened. Worth granting before a session that needs to.

**Delivery is intermittent even when the OS accepts the keys**, so a capture
showing no answer proves nothing on its own: one run reported "3 of 3 keys
played" and the game acted on none, the next reported the same and acted on
all three. Any null capture needs a positive control in the same run.

**A survivor keeps writing to the trace after the run ends.** `p.terminate()`
ends the launcher, not the wineserver's copy of the game, and a survivor holds
its `CX_LOG` open and appends raw lines to the file **after `distil` has
rewritten it** — so the log grows again behind the count `run` just printed.
Three menu captures came to hold gameplay nobody played: `menu-noinput` was
distilled at 3 events and later read 39. `run` now calls `kill_session()`
before distilling and warns if a process survives.

**The trace needs no shim, no patch and no debugger.** Every opcode handler
logs its operand through `Dbg_LogTagged` (0x0040EC70), which resolves it with

    GetPrivateProfileStringA("ZONES", "3732", ..., "IAM\ZONES.TAG")

and — this is the part that makes it usable — that call happens *before* the
`if (hWnd)` that would draw the text in the engine's debug listbox, guarded only
by `g_ScriptDryRun`. The window never has to exist. So the engine announces
every executed instruction's operand, with its domain, as an ordinary Win32
call, and Wine can log those. Confirmed from the running process too: every
captured line returns to `ret=0040ed42`, inside `Dbg_LogTagged`.

**Which binary — the correction that had to come first.** `Runtime.exe.asm` /
`.c` are named for `gamedata/Runtime.exe` and are **not of it**. The listing is of
**`gamedata/Runtime 2.exe`**, and three independent things say so:

* `gamedata/` contains that file's own IDA database — `Runtime 2.exe.id0/.id1/.id2/
  .nam/.til/.c/.h/.inc/.dif`. The decompilation was made from it, in place;
* its section layout is where every address in this repo lives (.text
  0x401000–0x4BC000, .rdata 0x4BC000, .data 0x4C0000), and Wine's loader maps
  it at exactly those addresses at run time;
* its VM dispatch table, read straight out of the file at 0x004C0140, matches
  `clean/_vmsummary.json` in **all 152 populated entries**.

`gamedata/Runtime.exe` is the **disc version's launcher** — 280 KB, four imports,
`GetLogicalDriveStringsA` + `RegOpenKeyA` + `VERSION.dll` + `MessageBoxA`: it
locates the install, asks for the CD and starts the game. Nothing in this repo
describes it. `verify.py: binary identity` pins the pair so this cannot drift.

**`PATCH.dll`.** `Runtime 2.exe` imports two names from it —
`DirectDrawCreate` and `DirectDrawEnumerateA`, and nothing else. It is the 2020
re-release's ddraw wrapper under a private name, it is not in `gamedata/`, and without
it the loader stops at `c0000135` before the engine runs one instruction. The
answer is an **export forwarder**: a PE whose export table points those two
names at `ddraw.*`. It contains no code, so it cannot change what the engine
decides, and it is written into the *bottle's* system32 — `gamedata/` stays read-only
input. `verify.py: golden-trace shim` reads the generated PE back through its
export directory the way a loader would.

**What CrossOver actually wants**, none of which is the documented Wine way and
all of which fails *silently*:

| the Wine way | what CrossOver needs |
|---|---|
| `WINEPREFIX=...` | `--bottle NAME`, or `cxstart --bottle NAME --` |
| `WINEDEBUG=+relay` | `CX_DEBUGMSG=+relay` |
| stderr | `CX_LOG=<path>` |

With `WINEDEBUG` the log comes out empty, which looks exactly like "the game
executed no scripts". Unfiltered `+relay` costs **111,283 calls to run
`cmd /c ver`**; `RelayInclude` (semicolon-separated, module name uppercase)
cuts that to 110 and leaves the game playable while recording. CrossOver's own
licence check calls `GetPrivateProfileStringA` as well, so the parser keys on
the *filename* argument and counts only `IAM\*.TAG` reads.

**What is invisible.** `Dbg_LogTagged` filters three things before it reaches
the profile API, and a diff has to apply the same three or it reports its own
filter as a disagreement:

    a1 == -1        returns immediately — nothing is logged
    "CHARACTERS"    resolved via Actor_FindById, printed from the actor
    "VALUES"        a bare number — there is no table to read

A capture is evidence about what *does* log and silence about the rest. Never
read a missing opcode as proof it did not run.

**First capture.** 25 seconds of the opening, three operand events:

    VARIABLES  175   Niveau Combat
    VARIABLES  170   Vie Combat Perte
    OBJECTS    997   ZVO P660 Vide Pas Traduire

Both sides now emit the same shape of stream, so the remaining work is to make
the comparison *fair*: a capture is a playthrough and a simulator run is one
entry point. The honest diff is per script — capture a single known trigger,
replay that trigger in `tools/sim`, diff the sequences.


**First real capture, and what it found.** 58 operand events over the opening -
new game, the Kay'l/Intro conversation, the Maelstrom cutscene, the walk, the
object grab - reading straight out as the game's own decisions:

    VARIABLES 19    Interface          AREAS     222   Anekbah Impasse
    CAMERAS   2148  cam Intro Arrivée  SCENES    55    1-01 Impasse
    DIALOGS   272   Kay'l / Intro      ADDRESSES 654   Arrivée Kumar

`attribute` then asked which script could have emitted each, and **25 of the 58
could be emitted by no slot in the corpus at all**. Following one of them found
the script: AREA chunk 118 holds `dialog.start 272`, `camera 2148`,
`camera 2152` contiguously, in exactly the order the engine announced them, at
a code block reached through the **relocated pointer at +68**. The chunk
declares 0 zone records and 0 second-table entries, so both existing walks are
correctly empty and the script is simply never enumerated. Conversation 272 was
one of the 106 with no known launch path; `verify.py: intro script` pins it.

Two wrong turns on the way, both caught by the rules in CLAUDE.md §1 and worth
keeping:

* the array at AREA +64, stride 44, lands **exactly** on the file size in
  188/188 AREA and 61/61 SCENE chunks, and four of its fields pointed into the
  code region. That is a strong data signal and it was **wrong**: `Area_Load`
  converts +0..+20 through the coordinate scale and +28/+30 through 360/4096,
  so the array is the **cameras** and those "pointers" were coordinates that
  happened to land in range. The data proposes, the loader decides;
* a byte-pattern scan for `3d 10 01` across `gamedata/` returns 250+ files - audio,
  morph, textures. Three bytes match everywhere by chance. Only the two script
  containers meant anything, and one of those, `gamedata/IAM/FRENCH/AREA`, is a
  **byte-identical copy** of `gamedata/IAM/AREA` (same MD5): the whole `FRENCH/`
  tree is a duplicate, not a second corpus.

**Still open:** of the 20 chunks that yield no slots (18 AREA, 2 SCENE) only
118 decodes from +68, so the other 19 - and the remaining 105 conversations -
are unexplained. The capture's other unattributed events (the Impasse scene
load, `ADDRESSES 654`, the Maelstrom shots) are the next thread, and they are
now *localised* rather than merely missing.


**Second capture: a location-triggered cutscene, and the first agreement.**
Walking into the trigger zone with the rig live gave the contrast that matters.
The engine logged, in order:

    VARIABLES/130  ZONES/78 "BOWIE OMIKRON THEME"  VARIABLES/132  VARIABLES/61
    CAMERAS/151    ADDRESSES/24   CAMERAS/152   CAMERAS/120   CAMERAS/121

`attribute` named it outright - **AREA 0 record 78**, 88 instructions, a
20-camera editing - and replaying that slot in `tools/sim` predicts exactly
those nine decisions, in that order, before continuing into the rest of the
script. The capture stopped mid-cutscene, so it holds a *prefix* of the
prediction, which is agreement: every event that exists matches. This is the
first time anything here has shown the simulator DECIDING what the engine
decided, rather than merely parsing what it parsed. `verify.py: bowie cutscene`
pins it.

So the two cutscene families separate cleanly under trace:

| how it starts | in the 5785? | replays in `tools/sim`? |
|---|---|---|
| walked into a zone (Bowie) | **yes** - AREA 0 rec 78 | yes, 9/9 in order |
| new game (the Kay'l intro) | **no** - AREA 118, unenumerated | n/a, nothing reaches it |

That is evidence the intro is reached by the *new-game path* rather than by any
script, which narrows the launch-path question instead of leaving it general.

**Determinism, the thing RECONSTRUCTION asked to bound before building.** Two
separate launches of a new game produced **55 of the first 55 events
identical**, diverging only where the player did something different. The
translation layer is reproducible at the decision level, which is the level
this rig captures.

**Three matcher bugs, all of which reported the tool's own limits as the
game's.** Worth keeping, because each looked exactly like a real disagreement:
requiring a script's events to be *contiguous* (the engine interleaves
concurrent scripts, so the match must be an ordered subsequence); requiring
*all* predicted events (a capture stopped mid-script leaves a prefix, which is
agreement); and replaying from `IAM\START` for a script the game ran later,
whose branches depend on accumulated state the trace does not carry. The last
is not fixed - carrying that state is what the rig needs next.


##### Attribution by runs, and why it is opt-in

The original rule names a script from a single event: a `(domain, value)` pair
only one slot in the corpus can emit. Sound, and wasteful — 150 of
`impasse-walk`'s 286 events are individually ambiguous and went unused.

A **run** is far more discriminating than any of its parts. `window_anchors`
grows a window from each position, intersecting the sets of slots able to emit
each pair; the moment the intersection is a singleton, that run names a script.
Across the four captures this takes attributed events from **221 to 381** and
distinct scripts named from **26 to 38**.

**The control is why it is believable.** On every event that also carries a
unique-pair anchor the two rules are compared — **221 comparisons, 0
contradictions** — and where they ever disagreed the unique pair wins, since
one fact beats an intersection. `verify.py: attribution reach` asserts the gain
and the control together, because the gain alone would not be evidence.

It is nonetheless **opt-in** in `diff` (`--window`), and that is a judgement
rather than caution. Turning it on finds more scripts to verify but also
produces disagreements that are not the simulator's:

* replaying `AREA 118`'s startup script from `IAM\START` takes the
  `Interface == 0` arm where the capture took the other — and anchoring on the
  save inverts it, because a save is the state at the **save**, not at the
  moment a given script ran. `telis-dialog` shows both halves: the engine took
  the *loading* path (`OBJECTS 753 "LOADING !!!"`) because a save was being
  loaded, and a sim seeded from that save takes the new-game path;
* a signature is a **static decode** — the union over a script's branches —
  while a replay takes **one** path. So a window can name a script *correctly*
  and the replay still predict a different branch, which the diff reports as a
  mismatch rather than as the "unreached" it is. `SCENE 57 rec 2 +4` against
  `impasse-walk` is exactly that, not a false anchor: the `CAMERAS/4434` that
  named it **is** in its signature, on a branch this state does not take.

So the standing `trace agreement` check stays on the strong rule, where "0
disagreements" still means what it says, and the run-based reach is measured
separately rather than folded in. A number that mixes real disagreement with
state fidelity is worth less than either.

> Worth keeping because it nearly went in the docs as fact: the first
> explanation offered for that mismatch was that the window had intersected
> the over-inclusive `wide` index instead of `tight` — which `indexes`' own
> docstring warns about, so it *sounded* right. It was wrong. The pair is in
> `tight` too, and the two indexes differ on only 84 of 6628 pairs. The
> anchoring was switched to `tight` anyway, on principle, but it changed
> nothing; the branch explanation is the real one. A plausible cause that
> matches a documented failure mode is still a hypothesis.

##### The third capture, and what it cost to read

`traces/impasse-walk.log` (2026-08-29) is the first that goes past the opening:
**286 events over 592 seconds** — the intro, the Impasse cutscene, the tutorial,
the crossing into the Sas, back into Anekbah for the whole BOWIE OMIKRON THEME
cutscene, then AREA 229 and into Kay'l's apartment (AREA 237, SCENE 57). Against
the two openings' 58 and 76 events, it roughly doubles the oracle's reach.

It replays with **21 scripts agreeing and 0 disagreeing**, and `AREA 0 rec 78 +0`
— the Bowie script — anchors **68** events where the older captures reached 9.

**Two things it exposed immediately, which is the point of a new capture.**

*A silent bug in the attribution.* `signatures` and `slot_index` both keyed a
script by `arch chunk rec +field`, and a chunk's zone records and its
message-subscription table are walked into the **same (rec, field) space** — so
`AREA 222 rec 1 +0` named two different scripts, the zone script at 2323 and the
subscription at 2463, and the second silently overwrote the first. Four of the
new capture's events looked unexplained (`ZONES` 3796, 3794, 3792) for no better
reason than that the `zone.enable` emitting them lived in the script that lost
the collision. With the offset in the key: **0 unexplained events**, and the
scripts the oracle can verify went 3 → 7 and 6 → 10 **on the two old captures
as well**. A key that is not unique does not fail loudly; it quietly verifies
less, and only a capture it could not explain made that visible.

*A rig limitation.* `run --seconds N` terminates the **launcher**, not the game.
The window stays up and the relay log keeps growing, so a capture is however
long the session actually ran — here ~10 minutes and 29 MB before `distil` —
rather than the number asked for. Read the file after `kill_session`, not
during: the event count moves while it is still being written.

##### Setting the rig up on another machine

    python3 tools/goldentrace.py bootstrap

One command, from a clean checkout to a working capture: find CrossOver, create
the bottle, arm the relay filter, generate `PATCH.dll`, build the run root,
then **launch the game and require operand events to come out**. That last step
is the point rather than a flourish - every failure this rig has had (WINEDEBUG
ignored, a stale session holding the wineserver, a missing CD marker) produced
an *empty log*, which is indistinguishable from "the game ran no scripts"
unless something asserts otherwise. Verified by moving the bottle aside and
running it from nothing: bottle created, 6 events captured.

**Nothing licensed is vendored and nothing is copied between machines**, for
two reasons rather than one:

* CrossOver is ~1 GB of commercial software and its licence lives outside the
  app bundle, so a copy would not run on the second machine anyway;
* a bottle bakes **absolute paths** into its dosdevices links - `y:` points at
  `/Users/<name>`, `d:` at the bottle's own path - so a copied bottle breaks on
  a different machine or a different username.

The bottle is therefore a **build artifact** (160 MB, ~2 minutes), not a
source. What is genuinely this project's is already in the repo: the tool,
`gamedata/`, and the distilled traces. `$OMK_CX` overrides where CrossOver is found
and `$OMK_BOTTLE` the bottle name, so a machine that keeps CrossOver somewhere
else needs no edit.

Two things the second machine still needs, and neither can come from here: a
CrossOver licence, and `gamedata/` (1.7 GB of shipped game data).


##### Carrying the state - what makes the oracle work past the opening

A trace carries decisions, not memory, so replaying a script needs a state to
replay it *against*. Three pieces, and the honest handling of each is what
turned 4 apparent disagreements into 0:

* **`--save`** reads a state straight out of `IAM\GAMES` slot n
  (`gamestate.from_save`). Exact - the engine's own serialisation of the same
  8192-byte DB - but it exists only if the player saved.
* **`--evolve`** carries one state across the whole diff, each replayed script
  writing into it in capture order. No save needed; only as good as the
  attribution, since a script the capture never names never runs.
* **anchors are validated against their own replay.** An anchor comes from a
  *static* decode, which walks every branch; the replay takes one. So an
  anchoring event can sit on a path the available state does not reach. That is
  a missing anchor, not a disagreement, and it is reported in its own column.

Two index-building errors were found and fixed on the way, and they want
opposite treatment - which is the finding, not a detail:

| used for | index | safe error |
|---|---|---|
| "no slot can emit this" (the residue) | the **superset** signature | over-include: a strong negative claim must not rest on a narrow reading |
| naming the script that ran (anchoring) | the **predicted** stream | under-include: naming a slot that would never log that pair invents a disagreement |

Anchoring off the superset made **5 of 10 anchors false on the walk-in
capture, and all five reported as mismatches**.

**Result:** across both captures, for every script named and reachable,
`tools/sim` decides exactly what the engine decided - 4 scripts on the intro,
5 on the walk-in, **0 disagreements**, with 2 and 4 respectively deferred as
unreachable from the new-game state. `verify.py: trace agreement` runs this off
the distilled traces, so it reproduces with neither CrossOver nor the game.

**Also settled in passing:** GAME_STATE §8 called the 3496-byte save header
unadjudicated, `SaveDir_*` walking 256 records of 72 bytes instead. A file the
engine wrote settles it - `3496 + 256*32808 = 8402344`, exactly the size on
disk, where the 72-byte reading needs 18432 of header and gives 8417280. The
header is not a directory at all: it opens with the profile name `OMK_SAVE` and
the display mode 640x480.

**And a fault of mine, fixed:** the run root symlinked `IAM` straight at `gamedata/`,
so the engine's saves were written THROUGH into the shipped tree - `gamedata/` is
read-only input. `IAM` is now a real directory of per-file symlinks with the
writable entries (`GAMES`) as real files in the bottle.

##### And what a running original is worth *before* any pipeline

The two questions currently blocking work do not need a trace at all — they
need somebody to look:

* the scene-clip **root orientation** (CUTSCENES 5): the floor test leans to
  the conjugate at 5.5 units against 6.0, which is a lean and not an answer.
  One screenshot of `1-01KAY.3DA` in the running game settles it;
* **what starts Impasse's beats** (CUTSCENES 5): nothing in the shipped
  scripts does, and more scanning is unlikely to change that. Watching the
  opening happen is the cheaper experiment.

Play has already caught two defects this session that no check here could see
(CLAUDE.md §1, "some errors are invisible at rest"). It is the highest-value
use of a running original, and it costs hours rather than weeks.

* **Deliverable:** `tools/sim/` — loader, state, VM, scheduler, interpreter,
  and a trace format — plus a `docs/GAME_STATE.md` from stage 2.
  [`GAME_STATE.md`](GAME_STATE.md) and its reader `tools/gamestate.py` are
  **written**; the state half of the simulator is `GameState`, and the rest
  of `tools/sim/` is still to come.
* **Then:** the port to a compilable engine becomes mechanical, and the
  simulator stays as the reference implementation to diff it against.

### Phase 5 — platform layer *(the rest)*

DirectX, win32, OLE. Identify and name; transcribe only if level C is ever
attempted. Lowest value for understanding the game.

---

## 5. Tracking progress

The numbers must come from the tree, never from memory.

```bash
python3 tools/status.py progress    # CLEAN / NAMED / READ / RAW per module
python3 tools/index.py              # regenerate readable/INDEX.md
```

Report progress **against game code**, and quote all four statuses — collapsing
them to one percentage hides whether a function was rewritten or merely named.

`tools/verify.py` **(written)** asserts every documented invariant and exits
with the number of failures, so "the docs say 1246" is no longer something a
later change can silently break. **88 checks, 84 of them fast** (the four
behind `--slow` are the whole-asset sweeps). It paid for itself
on the first run: 21 `#define` aliases that should have been real renames, and
a `.3DM` count of 708 that was really 777.

### Log

Append one line per session. Keep it factual.

| date | phase | what changed | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2026-09-04 | C | **The road traffic DRAWN, and drawing it found a bug the crowd had been surviving by luck.** `omk-play` stages a vehicle as the rigid body it is - no clip and no skeleton, so the sub-object is composed once at rest, re-centred on its own root (the four sit ~200 units apart in model space) and only turned and translated per frame, to `sub_437F80`'s `(x, y - 30.75, z)` and the heading `sub_453330` builds from the direction to its mover. **Watched**: a moto with its rider leaning on the handlebars, nose leading and the red tail light behind, and a hover taxi with its canopy, both riding above the road. **The bug**: `play.cpp` evicts from `charModels`, every frame, any model no STAGED ACTOR wears - so the 64 slots a bucket key addresses are not spent on the last area's cast. The traffic circuit's own bodies are not staged actors. `sli_fn` and `moto` are worn by nothing else, so the traffic drew on frames 0 and 1 and then vanished, its cached `CharModel*` pointing at a freed `std::map` node and `ready` reading false - which is why it disappeared instead of crashing. **The CROWD has the identical bug and is masked**: `PedStaged::mo` is cached the same way and the walkers survive only because a city's authored extras wear the same `PERSOS` models and hold them resident by accident. Fixed for both by adding the circuit to the eviction's `used` test. **And the first frame check did not catch it**, which is the row's real lesson. Reading `+16` as the effect id (the previous guess, labelled as a guess) gave one emitter a row and the wrong sprites; the block model gives **36 emitters and 2289 particles** for the intro. | 2026-08-30 | 4 | **The vertex shimmer implemented, and the table says what it is.** Mesh flag `0x8000000` was recorded as a "colour flicker" read through ramp tables. Reading `byte_4DDBB0` out of the disassembly settles it: **32 entries, 0 → +64 → 0 → −64 → 0**, fetched with **`movsx`** — a full oscillation about zero, so a *shimmer*, not a pulse. The set path adds it to all three channels through the identity clamp table; the clock advances **2 a frame, wraps at 256**, and the phase uses `clock >> 2`, giving a **32-step cycle over 64 frames ≈ 2.1 s**. The per-vertex term is `vertexAddress >> 4`, which steps by 2 per vertex on a contiguous 32-byte stride — reproducible as `2 × index` even though the address is not, so neighbours sit 1/16 of a cycle apart and the shimmer travels. Implemented in `/cutscene`: the phase rides as a 9th float per vertex (−1 = none) and the wave is a 32-entry uniform indexed in the vertex shader. **233 set meshes**, 0 characters — `berg*` 91, `fond*` 52 — and **Lahoreh has 132 of them, 15% of its vertices**, where Anekbah has one. Also separated: `sub_494180`, the *lit*-path variant, offsets through different tables and darkens as well as pulses, but no set mesh takes that path, so it is the actor variant and is left unimplemented. The struct is named by arithmetic, not by typing: `lea/lea/shl 5` is `eax*41*32 = 1312` = `ACTOR_STRIDE`, plus `g_Actors`. And the write is a **suspend**: a spoken line starting on an SCX-driven actor (state 4) parks the running object there and leaves state 5, which `Actors_TickAll` hands straight back to `Actor_StartPendingScx`. So `+176` is the save slot of a suspend/resume pair around a dialogue line, and **nothing can be parked there that was not already running** — it can only resume an interrupted beat, never start one. Exactly **one** instruction in the binary writes `ACTOR_STATE = 5`, so there is no second entry to hunt. The park gate is named on the way: **`Script_ObjectRunsForever`** (0x0044B460) — loop count `+52` = -1, or any sync-reachable function's repeat `+16` = -1 — so a one-shot program that would have ended is dropped rather than resumed.
